People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

Hi, thank you for seeing me. I’m not sure if this is the right sort of thing to come in for, but… I’ve been having trouble turning my worries off. Once something gets in my head—like my mother getting up at night, or a grant report, or whether I overlooked something at work—I can make lists and try to redirect myself, but I can’t really seem to shelve it mentally. It just keeps going.

I suppose that counts as tension, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it isn’t significant, because I am still functioning and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But… yes, I think it’s starting to interfere.\n\nAt the library I’ve missed some small but important details, things I normally would catch. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just sort of froze, and I had to hand off parts of it to someone else. That really isn’t like me.\n\nI’m a bit embarrassed saying that, honestly. It’s made me worry that maybe I’m not leading my team as well as I should be. Does that answer what you mean by interfering?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s not exactly the same, no. At work I can usually keep it more contained because there are people around and things to do, so I probably look more composed than I feel.

When I’m alone, it has more room to spread, I suppose. Over this past year it’s gone from mainly worrying about my mother’s safety to… well, finances, staffing choices, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel.

I don’t mean I’m panicking all the time, but it does feel like my mind keeps finding new things to attach to. Does that answer your question?

Even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel.\n\nI don’t mean I’m panicking all the time, but it does feel like my mind keeps finding new things to attach to. Does that answer your question?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't worry about anything at all?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean, there are days when it’s quieter, or I’m busy enough that I don’t focus on it as much.\n\nBut over this past year, it’s not really been nothing. It started with worrying about my mother’s safety, and then it sort of spread—to finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I don’t stay on top of them.\n\nI don’t mean that every moment is awful, but… no, I don’t think I often have a whole day where I’m not worried about anything at all. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it does follow me around, more than I’d like to admit.

It’s not always the exact same worry in every place, but the pattern is similar. Over this past year it started mostly with worrying about my mother’s safety, and then it spread into finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… things like that.

At work I may focus on the deadlines or decisions, and at home it might be the house or my mother, but it’s like the worry just changes shape depending on where I am. Even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel if I don’t keep watching them.

I hope that answers what you’re asking.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not completely, no… I’d like to say there were little breaks, and there probably were moments when I was distracted or things felt manageable.

But over the past year, the worry has kind of spread. It started more with my mother’s safety, and then it became finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I’m not careful.

So I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt totally free of it. Maybe quieter sometimes, but not gone. Is that the sort of thing you mean?
=====================================

Yes… most days, I think. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly falling apart, because I do get through things, but it’s been there in some form most days over the past year.

I suppose that counts as tension, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it isn’t significant, because I am still functioning and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But… yes, I think it’s starting to interfere.\n\nAt the library I’ve missed some small but important details, things I normally would catch. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just sort of froze, and I had to hand off parts of it to someone else. That really isn’t like me.\n\nI’m a bit embarrassed saying that, honestly. It’s made me worry that maybe I’m not leading my team as well as I should be. Does that answer what you mean by interfering?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about these things at the same level when you're alone versus when you're with others or at work?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same, no. At work I can usually keep it more contained because there are people around and things to do, so I probably look more composed than I feel.\n\nWhen I’m alone, it has more room to spread, I suppose. Over this past year it’s gone from mainly worrying about my mother’s safety to… well, finances, staffing choices, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel if I don’t keep watching them.\n\nI hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, even brief ones, where you felt completely free from worry over the past year?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I’d like to say there were little breaks, and there probably were moments when I was distracted or things felt manageable.\n\nBut over the past year, the worry has kind of spread. I think it’s changed… or maybe widened is a better word.

Over this past year it started mostly with worrying about my mother’s safety, and then it spread into finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… things like that.\n\nAt work I may focus on the deadlines or decisions, and at home it might be the house or my mother, but it’s like the worry just changes shape depending on where I am. Even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel if I don’t keep watching them.\n\nI hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, even brief ones, where you felt completely free from worry over the past year?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I’d like to say there were little breaks, and there probably were moments when I was distracted or things felt manageable.\n\nBut over the past year, the worry has kind of spread. I mean, her fall was stressful, of course, and the move changed a lot at home, but I don’t know that I can point to something else around that time and say, “That was the event.”\n\nIt felt more like… things started shifting, and I tried to handle it. Most of every day… I think, yes, though I feel a little uncomfortable saying it that strongly.

But… yes, I think it’s starting to interfere.\n\nAt the library I’ve missed some small but important details, things I normally would catch. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just sort of froze, and I had to hand off parts of it to someone else. That really isn’t like me.\n\nI’m a bit embarrassed saying that, honestly. It’s made me worry that maybe I’m not leading my team as well as I should be. Does that answer what you mean by interfering?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about these things at the same level when you're alone versus when you're with others or at work?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same, no. At work I can usually keep it more contained because there are people around and things to do, so I probably look more composed than I feel.\n\nWhen I’m alone, it has more room to spread, I suppose. Over this past year it’s gone from mainly worrying about my mother’s safety to… well, finances, staffing choices, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel.\n\nI don’t mean I’m panicking all the time, but it does feel like my mind keeps finding new things to attach to. Does that answer your question?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't worry about anything at all?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean, there are days when it’s quieter, or I’m busy enough that I don’t focus on it as much.\n\nBut over this past year, it’s not really been nothing. It started with worrying about my mother’s safety, and then it sort of spread—to finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I don’t stay on top of them.\n\nI don’t mean that every moment is awful, but… no, I don’t think I often have a whole day where I’m not worried about anything at all. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about the same things when you're in different environments or situations, such as at home, at work, or on vacation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does follow me around, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nIt’s not always the exact same worry in every place, but the pattern is similar. Over this past year it started mostly with worrying about my mother’s safety, and then it spread into finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… things like that.\n\nAt work I may focus on the deadlines or decisions, and at home it might be the house or my mother, but it’s like the worry just changes shape depending on where I am. Even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel if I don’t keep watching them.\n\nI hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, even brief ones, where you felt completely free from worry over the past year?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I’d like to say there were little breaks, and there probably were moments when I was distracted or things felt manageable.\n\nBut over the past year, the worry has kind of spread. It started more with my mother’s safety, and then it became finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt totally free of it. Maybe quieter sometimes, but not gone. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been present most days over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly falling apart, because I do get through things, but it’s been there in some form most days over the past year.\n\nAt first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and then it sort of spread into other things—finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Even little things can start to feel like they might unravel if I don’t keep on top of them.\n\nSo… yes, I think “most days” is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific traumatic or very stressful event that happened around the time your mother had a fall and moved in with you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there was one specific traumatic event, no. I mean, her fall was stressful, of course, and the move changed a lot at home, but I don’t know that I can point to something else around that time and say, “That was the event.”\n\nIt felt more like… things started shifting, and I tried to handle it. Maybe I’m minimizing it a bit, but I don’t think there was another major incident that I’m aware of. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying would decrease significantly if your mother's health and living situation were to improve or stabilize?\n\nPatient: Maybe some, yes. If my mother’s situation were more stable, I think that would take away one big source of it.\n\nBut… I don’t think it would disappear completely, if I’m being honest. Over this past year it’s sort of spread beyond just her safety. I find myself worrying about finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nSo I think it would help, definitely. I just don’t know if it would reduce it as much as I’d hope. Is that a reasonable answer?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying about different things changed over the past year, or has it remained relatively consistent?\n\nPatient: I think it’s changed… or maybe widened is a better word.\n\nAt the beginning, it was mostly about my mother’s safety. That felt like the main thing. But over this past year, it’s kind of spread into other areas—finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, things around the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I do manage, but even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I don’t stay on top of them. So no, I don’t think it’s stayed completely consistent. It’s gotten broader, I guess. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying about various things been present for most of every day over the past year?\n\nPatient: Most of every day… I think, yes, though I feel a little uncomfortable saying it that strongly.\n\nIt’s not like I’m panicking every minute, and I do get things done, but the worrying is there a lot of the time in the background. Over the past year it started with my mother’s safety, and then it spread to finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s been present most days, and probably for much of the day. Yes… I think I’d have to say it has, most of the time. I don’t mean that I’m constantly falling apart or anything, but it’s sort of there in the background a lot.

Over this past year it hasn’t stayed just about my mother’s safety. It’s spread into finances, work decisions with staff, deadlines, things around the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter. And sometimes even small things can start to feel like they’re going to unravel if I don’t keep on top of them.

So… yes, probably most days and much of the day. I hope that’s what you’re asking.

But over this past year, it’s kind of spread into other areas—finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, things around the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I do manage, but even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I don’t stay on top of them. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Yes, I think that’s fair to say. It seemed to start around then, when my mother moved into my home after her fall.

I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s not only about my mother anymore. I suppose that counts as tension, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it isn’t significant, because I am still functioning and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just sort of froze, and I had to hand off parts of it to someone else. That really isn’t like me.\n\nI’m a bit embarrassed saying that, honestly. It’s made me worry that maybe I’m not leading my team as well as I should be. Does that answer what you mean by interfering?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about these things at the same level when you're alone versus when you're with others or at work?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same, no. At work I can usually keep it more contained because there are people around and things to do, so I probably look more composed than I feel.\n\nWhen I’m alone, it has more room to spread, I suppose. Over this past year it’s gone from mainly worrying about my mother’s safety to… well, finances, staffing choices, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure it would completely stop, no. I wish I could say it would, because that would make it feel more… contained, I suppose.

It did start with my mother’s safety, but over this past year it’s spread into other things too — finances, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Sometimes even small things start to feel like they could unravel if I don’t stay on top of them.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. I’m not sure if this is the right sort of thing to come in for, but… I’ve been having trouble turning my worries off. Once something gets in my head—like my mother getting up at night, or a grant report, or whether I overlooked something at work—I can make lists and try to redirect myself, but I can’t really seem to shelve it mentally. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Did any of these worries start after something particularly stressful happened to you?\n\nPatient: I suppose it really picked up after my mother moved in with me, after she had a fall. That was… about a year and two months ago now. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because we’ve managed, but that does seem to be when I stopped being able to put things down mentally.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about a wide range of things beyond just your mother's health and your responsibilities towards her?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s not only about my mother anymore. Over the past year it’s kind of spread into finances, decisions with staff at work, deadlines, the house… and then whether I’m being a good enough daughter on top of that.\n\nEven small things can start to feel like they’re going to unravel, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things, exactly… or at least not in a big way. I still go to work and handle what needs to be handled. Maybe I put off some decisions or errands when I’m feeling especially wound up, but I’m not sure I’d call that avoidance. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your body, like tension or trouble sleeping, when you're worrying about these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, nothing terribly dramatic, but I do notice my body sort of holding it. By the end of the day my neck and shoulders can feel really tight, almost locked up, like I’ve been bracing without realizing it.\n\nAnd I catch myself clenching my jaw sometimes, especially when I’m answering emails or half-listening for my mother in the next room. I suppose that counts as tension, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it isn’t significant, because I am still functioning and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But… yes, I think it’s starting to interfere.\n\nAt the library I’ve missed some small but important details, things I normally would catch. And there was a grant presentation recently where I just sort of froze, and I had to hand off parts of it to someone else. That really isn’t like me.\n\nI’m a bit embarrassed saying that, honestly. It’s made me worry that maybe I’m not leading my team as well as I should be. Does that answer what you mean by interfering?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about these things at the same level when you're alone versus when you're with others or at work?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same, no. At work I can usually keep it more contained because there are people around and things to do, so I probably look more composed than I feel.\n\nWhen I’m alone, it has more room to spread, I suppose. Over this past year it’s gone from mainly worrying about my mother’s safety to… well, finances, staffing choices, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel.\n\nI don’t mean I’m panicking all the time, but it does feel like my mind keeps finding new things to attach to. Does that answer your question?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't worry about anything at all?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean, there are days when it’s quieter, or I’m busy enough that I don’t focus on it as much.\n\nBut over this past year, it’s not really been nothing. It started with worrying about my mother’s safety, and then it sort of spread—to finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I don’t stay on top of them.\n\nI don’t mean that every moment is awful, but… no, I don’t think I often have a whole day where I’m not worried about anything at all. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about the same things when you're in different environments or situations, such as at home, at work, or on vacation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does follow me around, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nIt’s not always the exact same worry in every place, but the pattern is similar. Over this past year it started mostly with worrying about my mother’s safety, and then it spread into finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… things like that.\n\nAt work I may focus on the deadlines or decisions, and at home it might be the house or my mother, but it’s like the worry just changes shape depending on where I am. Even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel if I don’t keep watching them.\n\nI hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, even brief ones, where you felt completely free from worry over the past year?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I’d like to say there were little breaks, and there probably were moments when I was distracted or things felt manageable.\n\nBut over the past year, the worry has kind of spread. It started more with my mother’s safety, and then it became finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt totally free of it. Maybe quieter sometimes, but not gone. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been present most days over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly falling apart, because I do get through things, but it’s been there in some form most days over the past year.\n\nAt first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and then it sort of spread into other things—finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Even little things can start to feel like they might unravel if I don’t keep on top of them.\n\nSo… yes, I think “most days” is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific traumatic or very stressful event that happened around the time your mother had a fall and moved in with you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there was one specific traumatic event, no. I mean, her fall was stressful, of course, and the move changed a lot at home, but I don’t know that I can point to something else around that time and say, “That was the event.”\n\nIt felt more like… things started shifting, and I tried to handle it. Maybe I’m minimizing it a bit, but I don’t think there was another major incident that I’m aware of. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying would decrease significantly if your mother's health and living situation were to improve or stabilize?\n\nPatient: Maybe some, yes. If my mother’s situation were more stable, I think that would take away one big source of it.\n\nBut… I don’t think it would disappear completely, if I’m being honest. Over this past year it’s sort of spread beyond just her safety. I find myself worrying about finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nSo I think it would help, definitely. I just don’t know if it would reduce it as much as I’d hope. Is that a reasonable answer?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying about different things changed over the past year, or has it remained relatively consistent?\n\nPatient: I think it’s changed… or maybe widened is a better word.\n\nAt the beginning, it was mostly about my mother’s safety. That felt like the main thing. But over this past year, it’s kind of spread into other areas—finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, things around the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I do manage, but even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I don’t stay on top of them. So no, I don’t think it’s stayed completely consistent. It’s gotten broader, I guess. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying about various things been present for most of every day over the past year?\n\nPatient: Most of every day… I think, yes, though I feel a little uncomfortable saying it that strongly.\n\nIt’s not like I’m panicking every minute, and I do get things done, but the worrying is there a lot of the time in the background. Over the past year it started with my mother’s safety, and then it spread to finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s been present most days, and probably for much of the day. I mean, her fall was stressful, of course, and the move changed a lot at home, but I don’t know that I can point to something else around that time and say, “That was the event.”\n\nIt felt more like… things started shifting, and I tried to handle it. Maybe I’m minimizing it a bit, but I don’t think there was another major incident that I’m aware of. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying would decrease significantly if your mother's health and living situation were to improve or stabilize?\n\nPatient: Maybe some, yes. So no, I don’t think it’s stayed completely consistent. It’s gotten broader, I guess. I mean, there are days when it’s quieter, or I’m busy enough that I don’t focus on it as much.\n\nBut over this past year, it’s not really been nothing. It started with worrying about my mother’s safety, and then it sort of spread—to finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I don’t stay on top of them.\n\nI don’t mean that every moment is awful, but… no, I don’t think I often have a whole day where I’m not worried about anything at all. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about the same things when you're in different environments or situations, such as at home, at work, or on vacation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does follow me around, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nIt’s not always the exact same worry in every place, but the pattern is similar. Over this past year it started mostly with worrying about my mother’s safety, and then it spread into finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… things like that.\n\nAt work I may focus on the deadlines or decisions, and at home it might be the house or my mother, but it’s like the worry just changes shape depending on where I am. Even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel if I don’t keep watching them.\n\nI hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, even brief ones, where you felt completely free from worry over the past year?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I’d like to say there were little breaks, and there probably were moments when I was distracted or things felt manageable.\n\nBut over the past year, the worry has kind of spread. It started more with my mother’s safety, and then it became finances, staff decisions, deadlines, things around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nSo I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt totally free of it. Maybe quieter sometimes, but not gone. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been present most days over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly falling apart, because I do get through things, but it’s been there in some form most days over the past year.\n\nAt first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and then it sort of spread into other things—finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Even little things can start to feel like they might unravel if I don’t keep on top of them.\n\nSo… yes, I think “most days” is probably fair.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific traumatic or very stressful event that happened around the time your mother had a fall and moved in with you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there was one specific traumatic event, no. I mean, her fall was stressful, of course, and the move changed a lot at home, but I don’t know that I can point to something else around that time and say, “That was the event.”\n\nIt felt more like… things started shifting, and I tried to handle it. Maybe I’m minimizing it a bit, but I don’t think there was another major incident that I’m aware of. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying would decrease significantly if your mother's health and living situation were to improve or stabilize?\n\nPatient: Maybe some, yes. If my mother’s situation were more stable, I think that would take away one big source of it.\n\nBut… I don’t think it would disappear completely, if I’m being honest. Over this past year it’s sort of spread beyond just her safety. I find myself worrying about finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nSo I think it would help, definitely. I just don’t know if it would reduce it as much as I’d hope. Is that a reasonable answer?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying about different things changed over the past year, or has it remained relatively consistent?\n\nPatient: I think it’s changed… or maybe widened is a better word.\n\nAt the beginning, it was mostly about my mother’s safety. That felt like the main thing. But over this past year, it’s kind of spread into other areas—finances, decisions at work with staff, deadlines, things around the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because I do manage, but even small things can start to feel like they might unravel if I don’t stay on top of them. So no, I don’t think it’s stayed completely consistent. It’s gotten broader, I guess. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying about various things been present for most of every day over the past year?\n\nPatient: Most of every day… I think, yes, though I feel a little uncomfortable saying it that strongly.\n\nIt’s not like I’m panicking every minute, and I do get things done, but the worrying is there a lot of the time in the background. Over the past year it started with my mother’s safety, and then it spread to finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s been present most days, and probably for much of the day. I hope that’s answering it the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying about various things been present for most of every day over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I’d have to say it has, most of the time. I hope that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying started as a direct response to your mother moving in with you after her fall?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to blame her or make it sound like I couldn’t handle it, because I have handled it, but that is when the worrying really began to be more constant. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying would completely stop if the initial stressor, your mother moving in after her fall, were fully resolved?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would completely stop, no. I wish I could say it would, because that would make it feel more… contained, I suppose.\n\nIt did start with my mother’s safety, but over this past year it’s spread into other things too — finances, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter. Sometimes even small things start to feel like they could unravel if I don’t stay on top of them.\n\nSo if that original situation were resolved, I think I’d feel relieved, certainly. But I’m not sure the worrying would just disappear entirely. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying would decrease to nearly none if all the current stressors in your life, including your mother's health and your work responsibilities, were to significantly improve?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think it would go down a lot, yes… if my mother’s health and work were both much better, I’m sure I’d feel more able to breathe.\n\nBut nearly none?
